1: # DriftWood — Implementation Plan
2:
3: Source: `driftwood-project-notes.txt` (compiled 2026-09-25). Repo state: greenfield (`README.md`, `LICENSE` (MIT), notes only).
4:
5: ---
6:
7: ## 0. Decisions
8:
9: | # | Decision | Status | Choice / default |
10: |---|----------|--------|------------------|
11: | 1 | **Tech stack** | **DECIDED (approved 2026-09-25)** | **Tauri v2 + Rust core + TypeScript web frontend (Svelte)**. Reasons: the river animation (feTurbulence/feDisplacementMap SVG filters) was already prototyped and validated in web tech — it drops straight into a webview; one Rust core crate gets wrapped by *both* the app and the MCP server (exactly the "two thin wrappers" requirement); rmcp (official Rust MCP SDK) is mature; small binary, MIT-friendly. Rejected alternative: SwiftUI + Swift core (more native feel and native `NSMetadataQuery`, but the SVG river animation must be re-created in Core Animation/Metal, and the Swift MCP SDK is less mature). |
12: | 2 | **Stage 2 default model** | Proposed default (vetoable) | Ship with a cheap-fast default (e.g. a Claude Haiku-class or GPT-4o-mini-class model via OpenRouter) and make the model a *setting*, not a constant. The prompt is model-agnostic; pick the final default during dogfooding. Alternative: hardcode one premium model — better reasoning, ~10× cost per scan. |
13: | 3 | **Cost guardrail** | Proposed default (vetoable) | Hard per-scan token/USD cap (default ~$0.50) with a themed "Snagged — ran out of river" state when hit, and always show estimated cost before a Standard/Deep scan. Alternative: no cap (bad for non-technical users). |
14: | 4 | **Band thresholds** | Proposed default (vetoable) | By *score quantiles over the candidate list* (top 15% of scores → Driftwood, bottom 15% → Source), with absolute fallbacks when the list is small (<20 candidates: use fixed score cutoffs, e.g. ≥75 / ≤25). Bytes-based banding distorts because one huge cache skews everything. |
15: | 5 | **Deep tier folder listing** | Proposed default (vetoable) | Top-level entry names + extensions only, depth 1, capped at 100 entries (then "+N more"). Names + sizes gets token-expensive and sizes rarely change the judgment. |
16:
17: Section 13's remaining items (weight tuning, model selection, threshold tuning) are dogfooding concerns — Phase 2 builds the CLI harness specifically to make tuning cheap.
18:
19: ---
20:
21: ## 1. Architecture
22:
23: ```
24: driftwood/
25: ├── crates/
26: │   ├── driftwood-core/        # ALL logic; no UI, no Tauri, no MCP deps
27: │   │   ├── scan/              # Stage 1: scope walking, Spotlight, hard rules
28: │   │   ├── score/             # drift score heuristics + banding
29: │   │   ├── reason/            # Stage 2: OpenRouter client, batching, prompts
30: │   │   ├── rules/             # local rule engine, memory folder, distiller
31: │   │   └── report/            # report model, grouping, serialization
32: │   ├── driftwood-mcp/         # thin binary: rmcp server over core
33: │   └── driftwood-cli/         # thin binary: headless scan for dogfooding/tuning
34: ├── app/                       # Tauri app
35: │   ├── src-tauri/             # thin wrapper: Tauri commands → core, event streaming
36: │   └── src/                   # Svelte frontend: onboarding, scan, report, theme
37: └── docs/
38: ```
39:
40: Rules of the architecture:
41: - `driftwood-core` is the product. App and MCP server must contain **zero** scan/reasoning logic — only transport, presentation, and permission plumbing.
42: - The core exposes two async entry points: `run_scan(config, event_sink)` and `apply_correction(...)`. Both wrappers call only these.
43: - Everything the UI shows comes from a serialized `Report` model; the frontend never reimplements grouping/scoring.
44:
45: ---
46:
47: ## 2. Data model (core types)
48:
49: **Candidate** (Stage 1 output): `{ id, path, kind: file|folder|app, size_bytes, kind_stats, last_used_date (Spotlight, optional), modified_date, orphan_status: orphaned|unknown|active, scope_category, score, score_components {size, age, cache_loc, orphan, depth, file_type, child_count}, band: high|middle|low }`
50:
51: **ReportEntry** (final): `{ candidate, tier: 1..4, tier_source: auto_high|auto_low|rule|llm, summary (one sentence: what it is / why it exists), reasoning (full LLM rationale for the dropdown), confidence, llm_model, privacy_tier_used }`
52:
53: **Rule** (rules.json): `{ id, ext?, folder_name?, parent_folder?, size_band?, orphan?, score_band?, tier, support_count, created_at }` — plain JSON, user-editable.
54:
55: **preferences.jsonl** line: `{ timestamp, candidate_features {ext, parent_folder, size_band, orphan, score_band}, original_tier, corrected_tier }`
56:
57: **ScanConfig**: scope category toggles (per Section 9 presets), privacy tier, model id, cost cap, rules snapshot.
58:
59: ---
60:
61: ## 3. Phases
62:
63: ### Phase 0 — Scaffolding (~half day)
64: - Cargo workspace + Tauri app scaffold (Svelte + Vite + TS), MIT headers, CI skeleton.
65: - Define the core types above and the JSON serialization in one place (`report` module) so both wrappers and tests consume identical shapes.
66: - `driftwood-cli` stub that prints a JSON report — this is the Phase 1–2 workhorse.
67:
68: ### Phase 1 — Stage 1 scanner (the biggest phase, ~1 week)
69: Order within the phase matters: walk → hard rules → orphans → score.
70:
71: 1. **Scope enumeration.** Resolve the three preset categories (Section 9) into concrete roots. Low: `~/Library/Caches`, `/tmp`, `~/Library/Logs`, orphaned `~/Library/Application Support`. Medium: `~/Downloads`, `~/Library/Containers`. High: `~/Documents`, `~/Pictures`, `~/Movies`, `~/Music`. Category is attached to every candidate — the report groups by it.
72: 2. **Spotlight-first metadata.** Use `mdfind` per scope root with `kMDItemLastUsedDate`, `kMDItemFSSize` attributes (`mdfind -onlyin <root> ...` + `mdls` batch for gaps). Fall back to a bounded `walkdir` for Spotlight-blind paths. Record *whether* last-used came from Spotlight or was missing — this distinction drives the "still in the current" rule (Section 13 mitigation: the rule is a hard filter **only** on positive Spotlight recency; missing data never blocks candidacy).
73: 3. **Hard rules.** Drop anything Spotlight says was used ≤ 21 days. Flag known junk roots (caches/logs/tmp) and compute orphan status.
74: 4. **Orphan detection.** Enumerate installed apps from `/Applications` + `~/Applications` (bundle IDs + display names, including nested .app inside .app for runtime-shipped apps). A support/cache/preferences folder is *orphaned* when no installed app matches its name under normalization: reverse-DNS match (`com.vendor.app`), vendor-prefix match (`com.vendor.*` counts as *active* — protects shared vendor folders), and fuzzy name match. Explicit never-orphan list: `com.apple.*`, known shared dirs (`Containers` group containers, `MobileSync`, etc.). This list is data, not code, so dogfooding can extend it.
75: 5. **Drift score.** Weighted 0–100 sum of the seven heuristics (Section 8). Starting weights (tunable via a TOML next to the CLI, not hardcoded):
76:    - size: 25 max, log₂-scaled
77:    - age: 25 max, days/365 capped
78:    - cache-location bool: 15
79:    - orphan status: 15
80:    - depth: 5 max
81:    - file-type histogram (`.cache/.tmp/.log` etc.): 10
82:    - child count (folders only): 5
83: 6. **Banding** per Decision #4, and **app bundles as units**: score a `.app` and its support/caches as one candidate where sensible (deleting an app means its data is what matters).
84: 7. Emit progress events throughout (files searched, bytes searched, candidates found, recoverable bytes — Section 11 counters) via a channel trait so app, CLI, and MCP all get the same feed.
85:
86: **Deliverable:** `driftwood-cli scan --scope low` prints a scored candidate JSON. This alone is dogfoodable.
87:
88: ### Phase 2 — Tuning harness (~2–3 days, overlaps dogfooding)
89: - CLI flags: dump score components per candidate, override weights, force band cut-offs, diff two runs.
90: - A tiny "known answers" fixture set (paths you *know* are tier 1/4) to check score sanity.
91: - Exit criterion: on your own machine, top-band items are all things you'd happily delete and bottom-band has no embarrassing mistakes. This is where weights and thresholds get their first real values.
92:
93: ### Phase 3 — Stage 2 LLM reasoning (~4 days)
94: 1. **OpenRouter client** in `reason/`: enforce `zdr: true` on every call (Section 9), plus provider routing preferences; streaming not needed (batch calls), but timeouts + 2 retries with backoff are.
95: 2. **Batching:** sort middle band by score, chunk 20–30 contiguous (score-proximity batching). Reference candidates by opaque `id`, never by raw path in the *response* format — the model returns `{id, tier, confidence, summary, reasoning}` per item, which prevents path-mangling and simplifies parsing.
96: 3. **Prompt sketch** (per privacy tier): system prompt explains the four river tiers, the still-in-the-current context, and the output schema; per-candidate payload is the structured metadata (Minimal strips paths/filenames and sends kind/size/dates/orphan/ext only; Standard sends full paths; Deep adds the depth-1 folder listing capped at 100 names). JSON-mode/structured output if the model supports it.
97: 4. **Robustness:** partial-batch parse failure → retry once → degrade failed items to their heuristic band label with `tier_source: fallback` and a themed "Snagged" note, never silently. Cost accounting per batch against the cap from Decision #3. Resume: persist batch results to disk as they arrive so a crashed scan isn't lost.
98: 5. **Report assembly:** group by scope category, per-group count + size totals, tier badges, sort within groups by score.
99:
100: **Deliverable:** CLI end-to-end scan → JSON report with summaries and reasoning.
101:
102: ### Phase 4 — App shell + onboarding (~1 week)
103: - Onboarding: three preset category cards with the Section 9 defaults (low ON, medium/high OFF), per-folder toggles, cache-specific "disable all cache scanning" button, and the **Full Disk Access explanation screen** — this is the first trust hurdle (Section 13), so it gets on-theme copy: what FDA unlocks, that DriftWood is read-only and open source, that only metadata leaves the machine (and nothing at all on Minimal). Include a live "detect whether FDA is granted" check with a themed retry state.
104: - Scan screen: "Search the river" button over the static watercolor art; Section 11 counters ticking live; status label pipeline (below).
105: - **Status label system:** core emits phase events (`Enumerating → Filtering → Scoring → Wading in (metadata) → Traveling to the river (LLM) → Sorting the driftwood`) mapped to the themed labels from Section 6; each label has a disclosure arrow revealing the raw detail (LLM thinking, current query, error text). Error → "Snagged", empty → "Clear waters".
106: - Settings: privacy tier selector, model picker, cost cap, "manage memory" entry point (Phase 7).
107:
108: ### Phase 5 — Report UI: the notebook (~1 week)
109: - Journal/notebook presentation: findings as field-note entries on paper-textured pages, grouped by category with group headers showing item count + size. Page-flip feel via CSS scroll-snap/pagination, **not** the rejected wash-ashore animation.
110: - Per entry: path (truncated middle), tier badge (Driftwood / Message in a Bottle / Current / Source — the Section 4 names), one-line summary, reasoning dropdown, **Reveal in Finder** button (`open -R` via Tauri shell), tier override control (feeds Phase 7).
111: - Report persisted to `~/Library/Application Support/DriftWood/reports/` so the last report survives relaunch.
112:
113: ### Phase 6 — River animation + theme (~3–4 days, mostly polish)
114: - Static watercolor river asset (from the Canva illustration) as the hero; layer the three *kept* effects from Section 7 on top: animated feTurbulence+feDisplacementMap wobble, pulsing shimmer highlights, paper-grain overlay at low opacity. All effects respect `prefers-reduced-motion`.
115: - Reuse the paper-grain filter in the report notebook background for cohesion. Explicitly do not build the drifting-specks effect.
116:
117: ### Phase 7 — Preference learning (~4–5 days)
118: - Memory folder exactly per Section 10: `preferences.jsonl` (append-only corrections), `rules.json` (distilled), `session-log.jsonl` (audit). Written under `~/Library/Application Support/DriftWood/memory/`.
119: - Correction flow: user overrides a tier → extract features (ext, parent folder, size band, orphan, score band) → append → background distiller regenerates `rules.json` (frequency-based: features shared by ≥2 corrections with consistent tier become a rule; support count recorded). No LLM involved.
120: - Rule engine: exact/normalized feature match against candidates in Stage 1 — a matching rule **pins the tier and skips the LLM for that candidate**. Hot-reload on file change. Rules are plain JSON and user-editable.
121: - Few-shot injection (privacy-gated per Section 10): only middle-band candidates with no matching rule; retrieve the N most feature-similar past corrections from `preferences.jsonl`; Minimal=never, Standard/Deep=opt-in toggle.
122: - MCP resources prepared here (same files the server will expose).
123:
124: ### Phase 8 — MCP server (~3 days)
125: - `driftwood-mcp` binary over rmcp, stdio transport (for Claude Desktop / any client).
126: - Tools: `scan(scope?, privacy_tier?, dry_run?)`, `get_report()`, `reveal(path)`, `correct_tier(path, tier, note?)`.
127: - Resources (read-only): `memory://rules`, `memory://preferences`.
128: - Progress surfaced as MCP logging notifications. The server enforces the same read-only guarantee — no tool exists that can delete/move/write anything outside DriftWood's own memory folder.
129: - Documented `claude_desktop_config.json` snippet in the README.
130:
131: ### Phase 9 — Hardening + packaging (~3 days)
132: - Manual test checklist: FDA denied mid-scan, network drop during Stage 2, empty scopes, 100k+ file Downloads, symlink loops (walk depth cap), unreadable dirs (skip + count as "wading past"), apps running right now (exclude active apps' data via `lsof`-free heuristic: last-used recency already covers most).
133: - Code signing + notarization (Developer ID) since FDA prompts are scary enough unsigned — even for personal dogfooding, notarization removes one frightening dialog.
134: - README: privacy model, ZDR enforcement, build instructions.
135:
136: ---
137:
138: ## 4. Edge cases / failure modes to design for now
139:
140: 1. **Spotlight disabled or indexing off** → scanner must degrade to walkdir + mtime and *label the report's confidence accordingly* ("the river ran murky — recent-use data was incomplete").
141: 2. **mtime unreliability** — never use mtime as "last used"; Spotlight date or nothing. Downloads folder is an exception (download date ≈ creation is meaningful).
142: 3. **Permission errors on walk** → skip-and-count, never abort.
143: 4. **Huge directories / child-count explosion** → cap per-dir enumeration, aggregate deeper levels into the parent candidate ("folder, 12k files, 3.2GB").
144: 5. **.app bundles & sandbox containers** — treat as atomic units; a running app's container must never be flagged orphaned.
145: 6. **LLM batch partially fails** → per-item fallback (Section 3.3.4), report marks `tier_source` honestly.
146: 7. **Cost cap hit mid-scan** → finish with heuristic tiers only, themed warning, no silent truncation.
147: 8. **Two DriftWood instances / scan cancellation** → single-flight lock file; cancel must be prompt (check the flag between batches, not mid-batch).
148: 9. **Path ambiguity in LLM output** — solved by opaque-ID round-tripping (Phase 3.2).
149: 10. **Never-flag list** — hardcode-banned: `~/Library/Keychains`, anything under an active profile, iOS device backups (`~/Library/Application Support/MobileSync`) always land tier 4 minimum regardless of score.
150:
151: ---
152:
153: ## 5. Testing strategy
154:
155: - Unit: score components, orphan normalization (the fuzzy matcher is the riskiest pure function — table-driven tests), rule matching, banding quantiles, privacy-tier payload redaction (assert no path fragments leak in Minimal).
156: - Golden files: `mdfind`/`mdls` output parsing against recorded fixtures (can't hit Spotlight in CI deterministically).
157: - Contract: LLM client against recorded response fixtures incl. malformed-JSON cases.
158: - CLI integration on real $HOME is the actual dogfooding loop — Phase 2 exists to make it cheap.
159:
160: ---
161:
162: ## 6. Why this ordering
163:
164: Stage 1 + tuning harness come before any UI because **the notes' biggest unknowns (weights, thresholds, orphan false positives) are all Stage 1 concerns**, and the CLI is the fastest way to iterate on them. The LLM stage lands before UI so the report content model is real when the notebook UI is built — no mock data passes through. Theme/animation (Phase 6) is deliberately after the report UI works: polish on top of functioning screens, never blocking them. MCP last because it's a thin wrap that benefits from the core API being stable.

(End of file - total 164 lines)
</content>
````

1: # DriftWood Backend — Status & Context for the App Team
2:
3: Status: **built and verified** (2026-09-26). All logic lives in `crates/driftwood-core`;
4: the app shell (Tauri) and MCP server are transport-only wrappers.
5:
6: ## Repo layout
7:
8: ```
9: crates/
10: ├── driftwood-core/   # ALL logic — no UI, no Tauri, no MCP deps
11: │   └── src/
12: │       ├── lib.rs        # re-exports + DriftError
13: │       ├── types.rs      # Candidate, ReportEntry, Rule, tiers, bands…
14: │       ├── config.rs     # DriftTuning (TOML), ScanConfig, default model
15: │       ├── events.rs     # ScanEvent enum + EventSink trait
16: │       ├── paths.rs      # base dir: ~/Library/Application Support/DriftWood
17: │       ├── engine.rs     # run_scan + apply_correction (the ONLY entry points)
18: │       ├── scan/         # scope, spotlight (mdfind/mdls), walk, orphan, hard_rules
19: │       ├── score/        # 7 heuristics + banding
20: │       ├── reason/       # OpenRouter client, payloads, prompts, batching
21: │       ├── rules/        # memory folder, rule engine, distiller
22: │       └── report/       # Report model, grouping, persistence
23: ├── driftwood-cli/        # headless binary (dogfooding/tuning workhorse)
24: └── driftwood-mcp/        # rmcp stdio server (tools + read-only resources)
25: ```
26:
27: ## The two entry points the app must call
28:
29: ```rust
30: run_scan(config: ScanConfig, sink: Arc<dyn EventSink>, handle: ScanHandle)
31:     -> Result<Report, DriftError>
32:
33: apply_correction(request: CorrectionRequest, memory_dir_override: Option<PathBuf>)
34:     -> Result<Vec<Rule>, DriftError>
35: ```
36:
37: - `ScanConfig`: `scopes` (Low/Medium/High categories),
38:   `privacy_tier` (Minimal/Standard/Deep), `stage2` (bool, LLM on/off),
39:   `model` (setting, never a constant), `api_key` (from env — core never
40:   persists it), `rules` (snapshot from the memory folder),
41:   `tuning` (`DriftTuning`), `persist` (bool).
42: - `ScanHandle::cancel()` — cancellation is checked between phases and
43:   between LLM batches (never mid-batch). Fine to wire to a Cancel button.
44: - Core enforces a single-flight lock (`scan.lock`, stale after 6 h) so two
45:   scans can't overlap.
46:
47: ## Event feed (themed status pipeline)
48:
49: `EventSink` receives `ScanEvent`s: phase changes, running counters
50: (files searched, bytes searched, candidates, recoverable bytes), notices,
51: and warnings. Phases map to the themed labels from the notes:
52:
53: | Phase        | Theme                    |
54: |--------------|--------------------------|
55: | Enumerating  | "Searching for driftwood" |
56: | Wading       | "Wading in"              |
57: | Filtering    | "Following the current"  |
58: | Scoring      | "Scoring"                |
59: | Reasoning    | "Traveling to the river" |
60: | Assembling   | "Sorting the driftwood"  |
61:
62: The CLI renders these to stderr; the app should map them to the Section 6
63: labels with disclosure arrows (notices carry the raw detail).
64:
65: ## Report model (everything the UI shows)
66:
67: `Report` → `groups: [GroupSummary]` (per category: item count + total
68: bytes) → `entries: [ReportEntry]`. Each `ReportEntry`:
69:
70: ```json
71: {
72:   "candidate": { "id", "path", "kind", "size_bytes", "kind_stats",
73:                  "last_used_date", "last_used_from_spotlight",
74:                  "modified_date", "created_date", "orphan_status",
75:                  "scope_category", "score", "score_components",
76:                  "band" },
77:   "tier": 1..4,
78:   "tier_source": "auto_high|auto_low|rule|llm|fallback|never_flag",
79:   "summary": "one sentence",
80:   "reasoning": "full LLM rationale (dropdown content)",
81:   "confidence": 0..1,
82:   "llm_model": "...",
83:   "privacy_tier_used": "...",
84:   "rule_id": "..."          // when tier_source == rule
85: }
86: ```
87:
88: Tier names (already in the model): 1 Driftwood, 2 Message in a Bottle,
89: 3 Current, 4 Source. `tier_source: "fallback"` must be surfaced honestly
90: (themed: "Snagged") — never hide it. Reports persist to
91: `~/Library/Application Support/DriftWood/reports/` (timestamped +
92: `last-report.json`).
93:
94: ## Behaviors the UI must honor
95:
96: - **Read-only**: no scan path can delete/move/write anything outside the
97:   DriftWood base dir. "Reveal in Finder" runs `open -R <path>`.
98: - **Still in the current**: anything used ≤ 21 days is filtered out,
99:   INCLUDING folders whose contents were touched (Spotlight-positive
100:   recency only; missing data never blocks candidacy).
101: - **Never-flag list**: Keychains, MobileSync, Mail/Messages data, group
102:   containers, etc. always land tier 4 minimum with `tier_source:
103:   never_flag`.
104: - **Tier override** → calls `apply_correction`, which appends to
105:   `preferences.jsonl`, re-distills `rules.json` locally (no LLM), and logs
106:   to `session-log.jsonl`. Rules pin tiers on the next scan and skip the
107:   LLM for matched candidates.
108: - **Privacy tiers**: Minimal sends no paths/filenames to the LLM
109:   (enforced by tests); Standard sends full paths, never content; Deep adds
110:   a depth-1 folder listing capped at 100 names. Show the user the model +
111:   tier used per entry; show cost before Standard/Deep scans.
112: - **Cost cap**: default $0.50/scan (Decision #3); when hit, remaining
113:   items degrade to heuristic tiers and the report carries a
114:   `cost_cap` warning ("Snagged — ran out of river").
115:
116: ## Verification done
117:
118: - 61 core tests + workspace clean build, 0 clippy warnings.
119: - Live dogfood scan on this machine (low scope, no LLM): 351 candidates,
120:   3.1 M files measured, 23.7 GiB recoverable; top band = orphaned caches
121:   (Epic Games Launcher, Kagi — genuinely uninstalled, correctly detected),
122:   bottom band = Apple/system data only.
123: - MCP server verified over stdio: tools `scan`, `get_report`, `reveal`,

125:
126: ## What still needs the design agent
127:
128: - Tauri app shell (`app/src-tauri`): wire Tauri commands to
129:   `run_scan`/`apply_correction`, stream `ScanEvent`s to the frontend,

131: - Svelte frontend: onboarding (scope presets, FDA explanation),

134: - No scan/reasoning logic belongs in the wrapper — the core is the product.

1: ================================================================
2: DRIFTWOOD -- PROJECT NOTES
3: ================================================================
4: Compiled: 25 September 2026
5:
6:
7: ----------------------------------------------------------------
8: 1. CORE CONCEPT
9: ----------------------------------------------------------------
10: - DriftWood is a macOS application / AI harness / MCP server that
11:   finds unused files, folders, and applications on a user's
12:   machine.
13: - Unlike a traditional rule-based file scanner, DriftWood uses an
14:   AI model to actually reason about whether something is safe to
15:   delete, instead of relying purely on static rules. This lets it
16:   catch nuance a rule-based tool would miss.
17: - DriftWood is strictly READ-ONLY. It never deletes, moves, or
18:   modifies anything itself -- it only reports. The user decides
19:   what to do with the information.
20: - Originally conceived for general/non-technical users (people who
21:   don't know what an MCP server is) as a storage-saving tool.
22:
23:
24: ----------------------------------------------------------------
25: 2. BUILD PLAN
26: ----------------------------------------------------------------
27: - Build the standalone macOS app first, for personal use and
28:   dogfooding.
29: - Build an MCP server version afterward, once the core scan engine
30:   is proven, so any MCP client (e.g. Claude) can drive it directly.
31: - Architect the scan engine and the interface layer separately from
32:   the start, so the app and the MCP server end up being two thin
33:   wrappers around the same core engine, rather than two separate
34:   builds.
35:
36:
37: ----------------------------------------------------------------
38: 3. WHAT A REPORT ENTRY CONTAINS
39: ----------------------------------------------------------------
40: For every file, folder, or app DriftWood finds, it reports:
41:   - The specific file/folder path
42:   - A button that opens/reveals the file or folder
43:   - Its risk level (see section 4)
44:   - A summary: what it is, what it does, why it was likely created
45: The user reviews the report and decides whether to delete anything
46: themselves -- DriftWood never deletes anything.
47:
48:
49: ----------------------------------------------------------------
50: 4. RISK LEVELS
51: ----------------------------------------------------------------
52: 1. No risk at all
53:    Example: a long-forgotten app's cache that regenerates unchanged
54:    if the app is redownloaded.
55:
56: 2. No risk, but maybe worth keeping
57:    Example: .dmg installer files -- you never know if you'll want
58:    them again.
59:
60: 3. Slight/no risk, but a higher chance you'll want it back, or it's
61:    annoying to redownload
62:    Example: an AI app's skill files -- redownloadable, but the app
63:    hasn't been opened in a long time.
64:
65: 4. Personal / lost data / non-replaceable
66:    Example: a 5-year-old Minecraft world. You might want it gone,
67:    but deleting it is permanent.
68:
69: Proposed river-themed names for these tiers:
70:    1. "Driftwood"            -- dead wood, safe to clear
71:    2. "Message in a Bottle"  -- technically disposable, worth a
72:                                   look before tossing
73:    3. "Current"               -- still in the flow, recoverable but
74:                                   costly/annoying to reacquire
75:    4. "Source"                -- the origin, irreplaceable, don't touch
76:
77: Previous candidates (archived): "Salvage" (sounded like "recover this"
78: but meant "annoying to lose"), "Anchor / Sunken Treasure" ("Sunken
79: Treasure" sounded valuable/keep, not irreplaceable/don't-delete).
80:
81:
82: ----------------------------------------------------------------
83: 5. THE "STILL IN THE CURRENT" RULE
84: ----------------------------------------------------------------
85: - If a file or app has been used within the last 3 weeks, it should
86:   not be considered for deletion at all.
87: - Exception: cases where deleting it does literally nothing because
88:   it's automatically recreated -- though at that point there's
89:   little reason to delete it anyway.
90: - Floated internal name for this rule: "still in the current" (i.e.
91:   it hasn't drifted free yet).
92:
93:
94: ----------------------------------------------------------------
95: 6. THEME AND UI
96: ----------------------------------------------------------------
97: - The entire app is driftwood/river themed, matching a watercolor
98:   river illustration (currently being mocked up in Canva).
99: - Main scan button: "Search the river" instead of "Search files",
100:   alongside an animated river visual.
101: - Report display: designed to feel like flipping through the pages
102:   of a notebook/journal rather than a flat list or table. Each
103:   finding could be styled like a field note / beachcomber's log
104:   entry to match the watercolor illustration style.
105: - Explicitly decided AGAINST a "results wash ashore" animation for
106:   the results list -- judged too hard to implement cleanly and
107:   likely to look bad.
108:
109: Themed status labels (replacing typical LLM status messages):
110:    - Waiting for LLM response      -> "Traveling to the river"
111:    - Running file-search commands  -> "Searching for driftwood"
112:    - Thinking/reasoning            -> "Daydreaming"
113:    - (proposed) Scanning progress       -> "Following the current"
114:    - (proposed) Fetching file metadata  -> "Wading in" / "Testing
115:                                              the water"
116:    - (proposed) Compiling final report  -> "Sorting the driftwood"
117:    - (proposed) Error/failure           -> "Snagged" / "Lost in the
118:                                              current"
119:    - (proposed) No results found        -> "Clear waters"
120: - A dropdown arrow next to a status label (e.g. "Daydreaming")
121:   reveals the underlying detail -- e.g. the full LLM reasoning.
122:
123: - Report grouping: results grouped by folder category (Caches,
124:   Downloads, Application Support, Documents, Photos, etc.) with
125:   per-group totals (item count, size) so users can mentally triage
126:   high-trust vs. personal areas at a glance.
127:
128:
129: ----------------------------------------------------------------
130: 7. RIVER ANIMATION -- TECHNIQUE
131: ----------------------------------------------------------------
132: Explored whether an LLM can generate a genuinely watercolor-looking
133: animated river purely via SVG. Conclusion: procedural SVG can't
134: really replicate hand-painted watercolor texture -- that's better
135: done as a static illustrated asset (like the existing Canva
136: artwork). Plan: keep the illustrated river as static art, and layer
137: lightweight animated effects on top of it.
138:
139: Effects tested and KEPT:
140:    - Turbulence-based ripple distortion (feTurbulence +
141:       feDisplacementMap, animated) for a "living water" wobble
142:    - Pulsing shimmer highlights (animated opacity on small light
143:       shapes)
144:    - Static paper-grain texture overlay (feTurbulence at low
145:       opacity) for a watercolor-paper feel
146:
147: Effect tested and REJECTED:
148:    - Small particles/"specks" drifting downstream along a motion
149:       path -- felt off, dropped from the plan
150:
151:
152: ----------------------------------------------------------------
153: 8. TECHNICAL ARCHITECTURE -- TWO-STAGE SCANNING
154: ----------------------------------------------------------------
155: You can't feasibly run every file on a disk through an LLM (cost,
156: context limits, speed), so scanning is split into two stages:
157:
158: STAGE 1 -- Deterministic, local, free (no LLM involved)
159:    - Walks the filesystem (on macOS, Spotlight metadata such as
160:      kMDItemLastUsedDate is much faster than statting every file
161:      individually)
162:    - Applies hard rules first:
163:        - Drop anything used within the last 3 weeks (the "still in
164:          the current" rule)
165:        - Flag known junk locations: caches, logs, orphaned
166:          Application Support/Preferences folders belonging to apps
167:          no longer installed (classic AppCleaner-style orphan
168:          detection)
169:    - Computes a deterministic "drift score" for each remaining
170:      candidate using weighted heuristics:
171:        - Size (bigger = higher score)
172:        - Age (older = higher score)
173:        - Cache-location boolean (in ~/Library/Caches, /tmp, etc. = higher)
174:        - Orphan status (orphaned app support = higher)
175:        - Folder depth (deeper = slightly higher, less likely active)
176:        - File type histogram (cache-like extensions .cache, .tmp, .log = higher)
177:        - Child count (many small files in folder = higher for folder-level)
178:    - Output: a scored candidate list (dozens-to-hundreds of items),
179:      each with structured metadata (path, size, dates, orphan status,
180:      drift score, component heuristics).
181:
182: STAGE 2 -- LLM reasoning, cloud-based (only runs on Stage 1's
183: candidates)
184:    - Candidates split into three bands by drift score:
185:        - HIGH score (top ~15%): auto-labeled "Driftwood" (tier 1),
186:          skip LLM entirely
187:        - LOW score (bottom ~15%): auto-labeled "Source" (tier 4),
188:          skip LLM entirely
189:        - MIDDLE band (~70%): sent to LLM for reasoning
190:    - Middle-band candidates batched by score proximity (similar
191:      ambiguity level per batch), ~20-30 per batch, to control cost
192:      and avoid context contamination from mixing obvious/ambiguous.
193:    - The LLM's job: reason about risk level and write the
194:      human-readable explanation for each candidate -- the part a
195:      rule-based tool can't do.
196:    - LLM receives structured data per candidate (path, size, dates,
197:      orphan status, drift score components, file type summary).
198:
199:
200: ----------------------------------------------------------------
201: 9. AI PROVIDER / PRIVACY MODEL
202: ----------------------------------------------------------------
203: - LLM reasoning happens in the cloud via OpenRouter, not via a
204:   local model. Reasoning: most regular users can't self-host a
205:   model, and models small enough to run on ~8GB RAM aren't good
206:   enough at this kind of reasoning.
207: - DriftWood will be fully open source under an MIT license, partly
208:   for trust/transparency -- though open source alone isn't
209:   considered sufficient for trust by itself.
210: - DriftWood will use/prefer OpenRouter providers with a Zero Data
211:   Retention (ZDR) policy -- providers that contractually do not
212:   store prompts or responses at all, not even briefly for abuse
213:   scanning.
214:      - OpenRouter supports this directly: a "zdr": true parameter
215:        can be set on API requests, and there's an account-level
216:        "ZDR only" privacy setting that restricts routing to
217:        ZDR-compliant providers only.
218:      - Plan: enforce zdr:true (or equivalent) on every DriftWood
219:        API call.
220: - Users will be able to choose different "levels of invasiveness"
221:   for how much data Stage 2 sends to the LLM. Proposed tiers:
222:      - Minimal: only structural metadata (type, size, dates, orphan
223:        status) -- no real paths or filenames
224:      - Standard: full paths and filenames, but never file content
225:      - Deep: for genuinely ambiguous cases, DriftWood can send a
226:        folder's file listing (not file contents) to improve
227:        judgment
228:
229: - Folder scanning scope / onboarding UX:
230:    - On first launch, onboarding screen presents folder categories
231:      with recommended presets:
232:        - "Low personal risk" (ON by default): ~/Library/Caches,
233:          /tmp, ~/Library/Logs, orphaned Application Support
234:        - "Medium personal risk" (OFF by default): ~/Downloads,
235:          ~/Library/Containers (sandboxed app data)
236:        - "High personal risk" (OFF by default): ~/Documents,
237:          ~/Pictures/Photos, ~/Movies, ~/Music
238:    - Each category has a toggle; user can customize per-folder.
239:    - Cache locations clearly labeled "on by default" with a single
240:      button to disable all cache scanning if desired.
241:    - Results always grouped by category in the report (see Section 6).
242:
243:
244: ----------------------------------------------------------------
245: 10. TRUST / FEEDBACK FEATURES
246: ----------------------------------------------------------------
247: - Users can correct a risk assessment they disagree with (e.g.
248:   "actually, always keep things like this"), and DriftWood should
249:   remember that preference going forward. Considered a strong
250:   differentiator versus other cleanup tools.
251: - The report should show real reasoning behind each risk call (tied
252:   to the "Daydreaming" dropdown revealing full LLM thinking), not
253:   just a flat badge, so the AI's judgment doesn't feel like a black
254:   box -- especially important for the higher risk tiers where a
255:   wrong call is costly.
256:
257: - Preference learning implementation (local-first, hybrid):
258:    - Memory folder (persistence layer):
259:        ~/Library/Application Support/DriftWood/memory/
260:          ├─ preferences.jsonl    # raw user corrections, one per line
261:          ├─ rules.json           # distilled deterministic rules
262:          └─ session-log.jsonl    # audit trail
263:    - Local deterministic rule engine (zero LLM cost, guaranteed):
264:        - When user overrides a tier, extract features (file extension,
265:          parent folder name, size band, orphan status, drift score band)
266:          → append to preferences.jsonl → background distill → update
267:          rules.json (e.g., {"ext": ".dmg", "folder": "Downloads",
268:          "tier": 2}) → rule engine hot-reloads.
269:        - Rules apply instantly to all future candidates (Stage 1 +
270:          Stage 2), before any LLM call.
271:        - rules.json is plain JSON, user-editable, exportable, portable.
272:    - Optional few-shot injection (privacy-tier gated):
273:        - Only for middle-band candidates that DON'T match a local rule.
274:        - Pull last N relevant corrections from preferences.jsonl
275:          (feature-similarity), inject as few-shot examples in LLM prompt.
276:        - Controlled by privacy tier: Minimal=never, Standard=opt-in,
277:          Deep=opt-in with folder listing context.
278:    - No fine-tuning, no cloud sync unless explicitly opted in.
279:
280: - MCP integration:
281:    - Expose memory://rules and memory://preferences as read-only
282:      MCP resources. MCP clients (Claude, etc.) decide whether to
283:      inject them into their own context.
284:
285:
286: ----------------------------------------------------------------
287: 11. SCAN PROGRESS / MOTIVATIONAL UI
288: ----------------------------------------------------------------
289: During a scan, show running counters for:
290:    - Number of files searched
291:    - Total size of files searched (e.g. "50GB searched" reads as
292:      impressive)
293:    - Potential space recoverable so far (sum of flagged candidates)
294:      -- arguably the most motivating number, since it's what the
295:      user actually cares about
296:
297:
298: ----------------------------------------------------------------
299: 12. SCOPE
300: ----------------------------------------------------------------
301: - For now, DriftWood is a one-off, run-on-demand tool -- not a
302:   recurring/scheduled background scanner.
303: - A future "ongoing companion" mode (periodic check-ins,
304:   notifications when driftwood piles up again) was discussed as a
305:   possible later feature, not something to design around yet.
306:
307:
308: ----------------------------------------------------------------
309: 13. OPEN QUESTIONS / NOT YET RESOLVED
310: ----------------------------------------------------------------
311: - macOS Full Disk Access permission: the app will need to request
312:   this to scan broadly, which even technical users find alarming.
313:   Needs a clear, on-theme explanation during onboarding --
314:   especially important for a non-technical target audience, since
315:   this is the first trust hurdle before any results are even shown.
316: - Spotlight kMDItemLastUsedDate reliability: false negatives for
317:   files opened via CLI, non-App-Store apps, QuickTime media, etc.
318:   Mitigation: drift score incorporates age + cache-location + orphan
319:   status as stronger signals; "still in the current" rule is a hard
320:   filter only when Spotlight reports recent use, not a requirement
321:   for candidacy.
322: - Drift score weight tuning: 7 heuristics listed (size, age,
323:   cache-location, orphan, depth, file-type, child-count) but
324:   relative weights not set. Will tune during dogfooding.
325: - Three-band thresholds: "~15% / ~70% / ~15%" — by count? by bytes?
326:   by score distribution quantiles? Decide during dogfooding.
327: - Deep privacy tier: "folder's file listing" — just names? names +
328:   sizes + extensions? recursive depth? Token cost vs. judgment quality.
329: - Stage 2 model selection: which OpenRouter model (Claude 3.5 Sonnet?
330:   GPT-4o? cheaper alternative?) — cost/quality/latency tradeoff.
331:
332: ================================================================
333: END OF NOTES
334: ================================================================

(End of file - total 334 lines)
</content>
```

## What I'm taking from the mockups

They're rough, but the direction is clear: cream watercolor-paper background, the river art bleeding off one edge, thin light typography, a soft blue pill CTA ("Search the river?"), and centered status labels with disclosure chevrons ("Traveling to the river ⌄" → "Daydreaming ⌃" revealing the reasoning text). I'll build on that into something more polished and structured.

## Design direction I'm proposing

- **Base:** warm cream paper (`paper-texture.png` tiled, assets blended with `multiply` so their baked-in paper edges disappear), ink-blue palette pulled from the river art, generous negative space everywhere.
- **Type:** a distinctive light serif for headings (field-journal feel) + a quiet humanist sans for UI text, both bundled locally so the app works offline.
- **Motion:** the three kept effects from the notes (animated `feTurbulence`/`feDisplacementMap` wobble, pulsing shimmer, paper grain), plus every text block getting staggered fade/blur/translate enter + exit transitions, page-level view transitions, spring-ish hover states on the stamps. All gated behind `prefers-reduced-motion`.
- **Screens:** Onboarding (welcome → scope cards with per-folder toggles → FDA trust screen with live detect/retry → privacy tier + cost preview), Scan (hero river, "Search the river" CTA, live counters, themed status pipeline with disclosure arrows, Cancel), Report notebook (field-note entries, stamp tier badges, reasoning dropdown, Reveal in Finder, tier override), Settings, plus the themed states: _Snagged_, _Clear waters_, cost-cap warning.
- **Architecture:** `app/` as a Vite + Svelte + TS frontend with a thin bridge module — it calls Tauri commands when running inside the app shell, and falls back to realistic mock data (shaped exactly like the `Report` model from backend-summary) so you can review the whole UI in the browser. No scan/reasoning logic in the frontend; the Rust wrapper is scripting's job.

## Before I build — the asset question

The existing nine assets cover ~90% of what I need (hero rivers, stamps for all four tiers, paper, ripple for "Clear waters", driftwood motif for the logo). Two additions would meaningfully lift the result, and per your workflow I'd like you to generate them before I wire the screens they belong to. **They're the only thing blocking me — the questions below I can default my way through if you'd rather I just proceed.**

**Prompt 2 — wide horizontal river for the scan hero** (desktop windows are landscape; the two river assets I have are square/portrait):

> A gentle river flowing horizontally across the frame from the left edge to the right edge, viewed from directly above, soft blue watercolor painting on cream cold-press watercolor paper, muted indigo and pale sky-blue palette with white shimmering highlight streaks along the current, painterly feathered edges, hand-painted watercolor texture with pigment pooling and paper grain, wide landscape format, most of the frame is empty cream paper above and below the river, no text, no border, no signature

10: #[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq, PartialOrd, Ord, Hash, Serialize, Deserialize)]

14:     MessageInABottle = 2,
15:     Current = 3,

177:

55:
56: impl ScanEvent {

59:             message: m.into(),
60:         }
61:     }

63:         ScanEvent::Warn {
64:             message: m.into(),
65:         }
66:     }
67: }
68:
69: /// Sink for scan progress. Called from async context; implementations must
70: /// be cheap and non-blocking (channel send, atomic increment).
71: pub trait EventSink: Send + Sync {
72:     fn emit(&self, event: ScanEvent);
73: }
74:
75: impl EventSink for Arc<dyn EventSink> {
76:     fn emit(&self, event: ScanEvent) {
77:         (**self).emit(event);
78:     }
79: }
80:
81: impl<F: Fn(ScanEvent) + Send + Sync> EventSink for F {
82:     fn emit(&self, event: ScanEvent) {
83:         (self)(event);
84:     }
85: }
86:
87: /// No-op sink.
88: pub struct NullSink;
89:
90: impl EventSink for NullSink {
91:     fn emit(&self, _event: ScanEvent) {}
92: }
93:
94: /// Collects events into a bounded channel consumer-friendly vec (tests).
95: #[derive(Default)]
96: pub struct CollectingSink {
97:     pub events: parking_lot_lite::Mutex<Vec<ScanEvent>>,
98: }
99:
100: mod parking_lot_lite {
101:     /// Minimal mutex substitute to avoid an extra dependency in tests.
102:     pub struct Mutex<T>(std::sync::Mutex<T>);
103:     impl<T> Mutex<T> {
104:         pub fn new(v: T) -> Self {
105:             Self(std::sync::Mutex::new(v))
106:         }
107:         pub fn lock(&self) -> std::sync::MutexGuard<'_, T> {
108:             self.0.lock().unwrap_or_else(|p| p.into_inner())
109:         }
110:     }
111:     impl<T> Default for Mutex<T>
112:     where
113:         T: Default,
114:     {
115:         fn default() -> Self {
116:             Self::new(T::default())
117:         }
118:     }
119: }
120:
121: impl CollectingSink {
122:     pub fn new() -> Arc<Self> {
123:         Arc::new(Self::default())
124:     }
125:     pub fn snapshot(&self) -> Vec<ScanEvent> {
126:         self.events.lock().clone()
127:     }
128: }
129:
130: impl EventSink for CollectingSink {
131:     fn emit(&self, event: ScanEvent) {
132:         self.events.lock().push(event);
133:     }
134: }

- UI/body: "Karla" or "Sora"? For minimal feel, maybe "Outfit" light? Outfit is geometric and modern. Field journal: maybe body serif "Lora"? Mixing: headings Fraunces, body Karla light — nice pairing, both Google fonts with variable fonts.

Key design decisions:

**Tokens:**

- --paper: #f4efe2 (from paper-texture)
- --ink: #1f2a33 / deep slate blue-black
- --river-deep: #3b5ba5 / #2d4f8f (from art deep blue ~ #2b4ea0)
- --river-mid: #7da4d1
- --river-pale: #cfe0ef
- --accent: #3f6fb5
- --bronze/amber for bottle tier: #c98a3e (stamp-bottle ring color)
- tier colors: Driftwood = slate/teal (#5b7d8f?), Bottle = amber/bronze, Current = mid blue, Source = deep green-blue. Actually use stamp ring colors: driftwood teal-slate, bottle amber, current indigo swirl, source deep blue-green.

**Layout:** full-window app, paper background, river art positioned per-screen. Large margins (8vw). Small caps labels with letter-spacing for metadata. Hairline rules (1px ink at 15% opacity).

**Screens flow:**

1. Welcome: big title "DriftWood" entering with blur/rise stagger, driftwood-motif faint behind or stamp logo, "an empty riverbank, sorted" tagline, "Begin" button. River vertical on right edge? Mockup 1 has vertical river center-right. I'll put river-vertical bleeding off the right side, title left-center.
2. Onboarding: three scope cards (Low/Medium/High) with per-folder toggles, defaults low ON. "Disable all cache scanning" button. Enter/exit animations staggered.
3. Trust/FDA: explanation, "Grant Full Disk Access" button + detect state (mock: pending → needs grant). On-theme copy. Retry themed state.
4. Privacy & cost: Minimal/Standard/Deep selector cards, cost estimate preview, model note.
5. Scan: horizontal river hero across bottom, "Search the river" CTA center; on start: CTA morphs/fades, status pipeline appears with current phase label + chevron disclosure showing notices; counters (files searched, GB searched, recoverable) ticking with animated numbers; cancel button ("Pull ashore"?). Phases: Searching for driftwood → Wading in → Following the current → Scoring → Traveling to the river → Sorting the driftwood. Mock simulation drives events. Snagged state on error; Clear waters for empty.
6. Report: notebook. Grouped by category with headers (count + size). Entries as field-note rows on paper page; stamp per tier (small circle image); path truncated middle; summary; expandable reasoning ("The river's reasoning"); tier override control (four small stamps to re-stamp → triggers correction via bridge); Reveal in Finder. Page-flip feel: CSS scroll-snap pagination with subtle page corners? Keep it simple: vertical scroll with snap sections per category + sticky group headers. Entry enter animations staggered fade-up.
7. Settings: privacy tier, model picker, cost cap, memory (rules count, clear memory), plus "run scan again".

Navigation: minimal top-left wordmark + tiny stamp logo; steps as a quiet progress "river path" during onboarding (dots connected by a curvy line?). Keep elegant.

**Bridge (bridge.ts):**

```ts
export interface Bridge {
  startScan(cfg): AsyncIterable<ScanEvent-ish>
  cancelScan(): void
  getLastReport(): Promise<Report|null>
  correctTier(...): Promise<void>
  reveal(path): void
  checkFullDiskAccess(): Promise<'granted'|'denied'|'unknown'>
}
```

Mock implementation simulates a scan with realistic timings and data. Detect Tauri via `'__TAURI_INTERNALS__' in window` — invoke('run_scan', ...) etc. Leave Tauri command names documented.

**Animations:**

- Global: `data-animate` in-view transitions via IntersectionObserver action (`use:reveal`) with stagger via `--i` custom prop.
- Text exit: Svelte transitions (fly + fade + blur via css filter) with out transitions on view switch (out-in).
- Numbers: tweened stores.
- River: SVG feTurbulence displacement animated via `<animate>` on baseFrequency or JS rAF; shimmer via CSS animated opacity on highlight layers (use the horizontal river asset twice: base + displaced). Simplest robust: wrap river img in SVG filter; animate seed/baseFrequency slowly. Respect reduced motion.
- Paper grain: CSS overlay using paper texture at low opacity multiply + feTurbulence noise SVG as data URI... The paper-texture asset itself is the background; plus a subtle vignette.

Report overrides: call bridge.correctTier → toast "The river will remember that." (preference learning themed).

Tier override UI: small row of four mini-stamps; selected has ink ring.

Time to build. This is a big build. Steps:

1. Scaffold files manually (fast, no `npm create` interactivity).
2. npm install (svelte, vite, ts, fontsource).
3. Copy assets into app/public/assets (rename "river horizontal.png" → river-horizontal.png).
4. Build components & screens.
5. npm run dev + check for errors; maybe screenshot? I can't screenshot easily... Could use a headless browser? There might not be one. I'll at least ensure build passes (`vite build`).

Motion primitives:

- View transitions: Svelte custom transitions `paperIn`/`paperOut`: y 24px, blur 8px, duration 600ms, easing cubicOut; out 300ms.

export type Band='high'|'middle'|'low';
export type Phase='enumerating'|'wading'|'filtering'|'scoring'|'reasoning'|'assembling';

export interface Candidate {...}
export interface ReportEntry {...}
export interface ReportGroup { category: ScopeCategory; count:number; bytes:number }
export interface Report { groups: ReportGroup[]; entries: ReportEntry[]; warnings?: string[]; cost_cap?: boolean }
export interface ScanConfig { scopes: ScopeCategory[]; privacy_tier: PrivacyTier; stage2: boolean; model?: string; cost_cap_usd?: number }
export type ScanEvent = {type:'phase', phase:Phase}|{type:'files_searched',total:number}|{type:'bytes_searched',total:number}|{type:'candidates_found',total:number}|{type:'recoverable_bytes',total:number}|{type:'notice',message:string}|{type:'warn',message:string}|{type:'error',message:string}

{
  "content": "export function formatBytes(bytes: number, precision?: number): string {\n  if (bytes === 0) return \"0 B\";\n  const units = [\"B\", \"KB\", \"MB\", \"GB\", \"TB\"];\n  const i = Math.min(\n    Math.floor(Math.log(Math.abs(bytes)) / Math.log(1024)),\n    units.length - 1,\n  );\n  const value = bytes / Math.pow(1024, i);\n  const digits = precision ?? (i === 0 ? 0 : value >= 100 ? 0 : value >= 10 ? 1 : 2);\n  return `${value.toFixed(digits)} ${units[i]}`;\n}\n\nexport function formatCount(n: number): string {\n  return new Intl.NumberFormat(\"en-US\").format(Math.round(n));\n}\n\nexport function truncateMiddle(path: string, max = 48): string {\n  if (path.length <= max) return path;\n  const head = Math.ceil((max - 1) / 2);\n  const tail = Math.floor((max - 1) / 2);\n  return path.slice(0, head) + \"…\" + path.slice(path.length - tail);\n}\n\nexport function fileName(path: string): string {\n  const parts = path.split(\"/\");\n  return parts[parts.length - 1] || path;\n}\n\nexport function relDate(iso?: string): string {\n  if (!iso) return \"date unknown\";\n  const then = new Date(iso).getTime();\n  const days = Math.floor((Date.now() - then) / 86_400_000);\n  if (days <= 0) return \"today\";\n  if (days === 1) return \"yesterday\";\n  if (days < 31) return `${days} days ago`;\n  const months = Math.round(days / 30.4);\n  if (months < 18) return `${months} month${months === 1 ? \"\" : \"s\"} ago`;\n  const years = +(days / 365).toFixed(1);\n  return `${years} years ago`;\n}\n\nexport function confidenceWord(c: number): string {\n  if (c >= 0.9) return \"certain\";\n  if (c >= 0.75) return \"confident\";\n  if (c >= 0.55) return \"fairly sure\";\n  return \"uncertain\";\n}\n",

  "content": "import type { Action } from \"svelte/action\";\n\nexport const reducedMotion =\n  typeof window !== \"undefined\" &&\n  window.matchMedia(\"(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)\").matches;\n\n/**\n * Reveal-on-enter. Add `class=\"rv\"` and optionally `style=\"--rv-delay: 120ms\"`.\n * The element fades/rises/declutters the first time it enters the viewport.\n */\nexport const reveal: Action<HTMLElement, number | undefined> = (el, delay) => {\n  if (delay !== undefined) el.style.setProperty(\"--rv-delay\", `${delay}ms`);\n  // Elements already in view on first paint still animate, just sooner.\n  const io = new IntersectionObserver(\n    (entries) => {\n      for (const e of entries) {\n        if (e.isIntersecting) {\n          el.classList.add(\"is-in\");\n          io.disconnect();\n        }\n      }\n    },\n    { threshold: 0.05, rootMargin: \"0px 0px -4% 0px\" },\n  );\n  io.observe(el);\n  return { destroy: () => io.disconnect() };\n};\n\n/** Delay helper for stagger chains (ms). */\nexport const stagger = (i: number, step = 70, base = 0) => base + i * step;\n\nexport function clamp(v: number, min: number, max: number): number {\n  return Math.min(max, Math.max(min, v));\n}\n",

{
  "content": "import type { Report, ScanConfig, ScanEvent, Tier } from \"./types\";\nimport { MOCK_REPORT, mockScanEvents } from \"./mock\";\n\n/**\n * The only seam between the frontend and the world.\n *\n * Inside the Tauri shell these map to Rust commands (see app/src-tauri when\n * the scripting layer lands):\n *   check_full_disk_access() -> \"granted\" | \"denied\" | \"unknown\"\n *   open_system_settings()\n *   start_scan(config)            — emits `scan-event` windows\n *   cancel_scan()\n *   last_report() -> Report | null\n *   correct_tier(id, tier, note?)\n *   reveal_path(path)             — `open -R <path>`\n * In the browser (and until the shell is wired) a mock bridge answers, so\n * every screen is fully reviewable in `npm run dev`.\n */\n\nexport type FdaStatus = \"granted\" | \"denied\" | \"unknown\";\n\nexport interface Bridge {\n  readonly name: string;\n  checkFullDiskAccess(): Promise<FdaStatus>;\n  openSystemSettings(): void;\n  startScan(cfg: ScanConfig, onEvent: (e: ScanEvent) => void): Promise<Report>;\n  cancelScan(): void;\n  getLastReport(): Promise<Report | null>;\n  correctTier(id: string, tier: Tier): Promise<void>;\n  reveal(path: string): void;\n}\n\nconst w = () => window as unknown as {\n  __TAURI_INTERNALS__?: { invoke: (cmd: string, args?: unknown) => Promise<unknown> };\n};\n\nfunction tauriInvoke<T>(cmd: string, args?: Record<string, unknown>): Promise<T> {\n  const internals = w().__TAURI_INTERNALS__;\n  if (!internals) return Promise.reject(new Error(\"not inside the Tauri shell\"));\n  return internals.invoke(cmd, args) as Promise<T>;\n}\n\nclass TauriBridge implements Bridge {\n  readonly name = \"tauri\";\n  async checkFullDiskAccess(): Promise<FdaStatus> {\n    return tauriInvoke<FdaStatus>(\"check_full_disk_access\");\n  }\n  openSystemSettings(): void {\n    void tauriInvoke(\"open_system_settings\");\n  }\n  async startScan(cfg: ScanConfig, onEvent: (e: ScanEvent) => void): Promise<Report> {\n    const unlisten = await tauriInvoke<() => void>(\"__listen_scan_events__\", {}).catch(\n      () => null,\n    );\n    // The shell wires `scan-event` windows to the core event feed.\n    const { listen } = await import(\"./tauri-events\");\n    const off = await listen<ScanEvent>(\"scan-event\", onEvent);\n    try {\n      return await tauriInvoke<Report>(\"start_scan\", { config: cfg });\n    } finally {\n      off?.();\n      unlisten?.();\n    }\n  }\n  cancelScan(): void {\n    void tauriInvoke(\"cancel_scan\");\n  }\n  async getLastReport(): Promise<Report | null> {\n    return tauriInvoke<Report | null>(\"last_report\");\n  }\n  async correctTier(id: string, tier: Tier): Promise<void> {\n    await tauriInvoke(\"correct_tier\", { id, tier });\n  }\n  reveal(path: string): void {\n    void tauriInvoke(\"reveal_path\", { path });\n  }\n}\n\nclass MockBridge implements Bridge {\n  readonly name = \"mock\";\n  private cancelled = false;\n  private failScan = false;\n\n  constructor() {\n    if (typeof window !== \"undefined\") {\n      const q = new URLSearchParams(window.location.search);\n      this.failScan = q.has(\"snag\");\n    }\n  }\n\n  async checkFullDiskAccess(): Promise<FdaStatus> {\n    await sleep(1_100);\n    return \"denied\";\n  }\n  openSystemSettings(): void {\n    /* mock — would deep-link to x-apple.systempreferences */\n  }\n  async startScan(cfg: ScanConfig, onEvent: (e: ScanEvent) => void): Promise<Report> {\n    this.cancelled = false;\n    const script = mockScanEvents(cfg, this.failScan);\n    for (const [t, event] of script) {\n      await sleep(t === 0 ? 0 : t - (script[script.indexOf([t, event])]?.[0] ?? 0));\n      // schedule by absolute offsets instead:\n    }\n    // (absolute-offset scheduling, cleanly:)\n    let elapsed = 0;\n    for (const [t, event] of script) {\n      const wait = t - elapsed;\n      if (wait > 0) await sleep(wait);\n      elapsed = t;\n      if (this.cancelled) throw new Error(\"cancelled\");\n      onEvent(event);\n      if (event.type === \"error\") throw new Error(event.message);\n    }\n    if (this.cancelled) throw new Error(\"cancelled\");\n    return MOCK_REPORT;\n  }\n  cancelScan(): void {\n    this.cancelled = true;\n  }\n  async getLastReport(): Promise<Report | null> {\n    return MOCK_REPORT;\n  }\n  async correctTier(): Promise<void> {\n    await sleep(500);\n  }\n  reveal(): void {\n    /* mock — would run `open -R <path>` */\n  }\n}\n\nfunction sleep(ms: number) {\n  return new Promise((r) => setTimeout(r, ms));\n}\n\nexport const bridge: Bridge =\n  typeof window !== \"undefined\" && \"__TAURI_INTERNALS__\" in window\n    ? new TauriBridge()\n    : new MockBridge();\n",

{
  "content": "/* Minimal Tauri v2 event listener shim, kept dependency-free.\n   Uses __TAURI_INTERNALS__ the same way @tauri-apps/api does under the hood. */\n\ntype UnlistenFn = () => void;\n\ninterface OnceFlag {\n  (payload: unknown): void;\n}\n\nexport async function listen<T>(\n  event: string,\n  handler: (payload: T) => void,\n): Promise<UnlistenFn> {\n  const internals = (\n    window as unknown as {\n      __TAURI_INTERNALS__?: {\n        invoke: (cmd: string, args?: unknown) => Promise<unknown>;\n        transformCallback?: (cb: (e: { payload: unknown }) => void) => number;\n      };\n    }\n  ).__TAURI_INTERNALS__;\n  if (!internals) return () => {};\n\n  const transform = internals.transformCallback ?? ((cb: OnceFlag) => cb as unknown as number);\n  const handlerId = transform((e) => handler((e as { payload: T }).payload));\n\n  await internals.invoke(\"plugin:event|listen\", {\n    event,\n    target: { kind: \"Any\" },\n    handler: handlerId,\n  });\n\n  return () => {\n    void internals.invoke(\"plugin:event|unlisten\", {\n      event,\n      eventId: handlerId,\n    });\n  };\n}\n",

Apply `mix-blend-mode: multiply` on the img so its cream bg multiplies onto paper (slightly darkens where overlapped — acceptable) — actually multiply with same cream gives cream×cream = slightly darker cream. Implementation:

```svelte
<div class="river {variant}" style:--intensity>
  <svg class="fx" width="0" height="0"><filter id={fid} x="-5%" y="-5%" width="110%" height="110%">
    <feTurbulence type="fractalNoise" baseFrequency="0.009 0.014" numOctaves="2" seed="7" result="w">
      <animate attributeName="baseFrequency" dur="24s" values="0.009 0.014;0.012 0.010;0.009 0.014" repeatCount="indefinite"/>
    </feTurbulence>
    <feDisplacementMap in="SourceGraphic" in2="w" scale={intensity} xChannelSelector="R" yChannelSelector="G"/>
  </filter></svg>
  <img class="base" src={src} alt="" style:filter={`url(#${fid})`}/>
  <img class="shimmer" src={src} alt=""/>
</div>
```

Note: animating scale prop changes filter → re-render fine.

CSS: .river{position:absolute; inset:0; pointer-events:none} .base{position:absolute;...} per variant sizing:

- horizontal: bottom:-2%; left:0; width:100%; height:62%; object-fit:cover; mask top feather.
- vertical: right:-6%; top:0; height:105%; width:min(48vw,760px); object-fit:cover; mask left feather + bottom feather.
- diagonal: right:-8%; top:-4%; height:110%; width:min(44vw,700px); mask left+top.

Shimmer img same geometry: copy via same class .layer, then .shimmer { mix-blend-mode: lighten; opacity:.0→? animation shimmer 7s ease-in-out infinite alternate; filter: brightness(1.18) blur(0.5px);} base opacity of shimmer .12 max.

Wait: lighten blend of a cream bg over paper: cream (247,242,230) vs paper (243,237,222) → lighten picks max per channel → nearly identical, so bg stays invisible.

Also add a slow whole-layer drift animation: transform: translateX(±8px) translateY(±4px) scale(1.01) 34s alternate. Subtle.

Reduced motion: media query kills SMIL? SMIL isn't CSS-controlled. Conditionally render <animate> based on reducedMotion flag from motion.ts. Also intensity 0 disables displacement entirely.

_Thinking:_

Issue: `const s = $onboarding;` — reading store into const: in runes mode `$onboarding` is reactive compiler magic; assigning to const loses reactivity? Actually `$onboarding` compiles to a reactive reference; `const s = $onboarding` — I believe this makes s a snapshot that... In Svelte 5, `const x = $store` captures current value and is NOT reactive (it's just a value read). Reactive usage must keep `$onboarding` inline. `const st = $scan` same problem.

Use `$derived` to keep reactive: `const s = $derived($onboarding)` — allowed. Or use store subscription inline. Let me fix both Scan.svelte usages:

- `const s = $derived($onboarding);`
- `const st = $derived($scan);`

Also `cfg` uses `$derived.by<ScanConfig>` — generic param allowed? `$derived.by(fn)` — typing: `$derived.by(() => expr)`. The generic form `$derived.by<ScanConfig>(...)` hmm, $derived is a rune function; type param should be fine in TS? Actually rune `$derived`isn't a real function — compiler transform.`$derived.by` typed as `by<T>(fn: () => T): T`. Generic ok. But I used `$derived.by<ScanConfig>(() => {...})` — fine.

Simpler: `const cfg = $derived(buildConfig($onboarding))`. Keep as is but change s.

## Assistant (Design · GLM-5.3-Flash · 46.6s)

_Thinking:_

Issues in Settings:

1. `<script context="module">` — should be `<script module>` in Svelte 5; and function used before definition — hoisting works for function declarations. But context="module" syntax is Svelte 4; Svelte 5 uses `<script module>`. Simpler: drop module script, define toastSoon locally as no-op with comment.
2. bind:value={DEFAULT_SETTINGS.model} — binding to a plain object export; works but won't persist. Better local state + save. Let me restructure: local `model = $state(DEFAULT_SETTINGS.model)` and onchange save to localStorage? DEFAULT_SETTINGS isn't persisted. Keep simple: local state; persist via saveOnboarding? Model isn't part of OnboardingState type. Add optional fields? Eh — simplest: keep DEFAULT_SETTINGS mutable object (module-level) and bind to local $state copy; no persistence claim. Fine for v1 UI.

Also setCap mutating DEFAULT_SETTINGS then `s = {...s}` is weird. Redo cleanly.

import type { TransitionConfig } from "svelte/transition";
const pageIn = (
  node: Element,
  { delay = 0 }: { delay?: number },
): TransitionConfig => ({
  delay,
  duration: 620,
  easing: cubicOut,
  css: (t, u) =>
    `opacity:${t}; transform: translateY(${26 * u}px); filter: blur(${8 * u}px)`,
});
const pageOut = (node) => ({
  duration: 260,
  easing: cubicIn,
  css: (t, u) =>
    `opacity:${t}; transform: translateY(${10 * (1 - t)}px); filter: blur(${5 * (1 - t)}px)`,
});

## Assistant (Design · GLM-5.3-Flash · 26.4s)

_Thinking:_

The report screenshot approach won't work — the store needs report data which only populates after a mock scan. Better: in the shot script, from scan view, click "Search the river" and wait for mock scan (~16s at low scope scale 0.85 → ~14s). Then screenshot scan-running mid-way and report after.

Let me rewrite the script: goto ?view=scan with seeded localStorage (need to set localStorage before page scripts run: use context.addInitScript). Then:

1. scan-idle screenshot.
2. click CTA, wait 4s → screenshot scan-running.
3. wait total ~20s → report screenshot.
4. scroll report to a group, screenshot report-2.
5. click a "Why the river says so" → screenshot expanded.

**Tool: write**

**Input:**

```json
{
  "content": "import { chromium } from \"playwright-core\";\n\nconst seed = JSON.stringify({\n  scopes: { low: true, medium: false, high: false },\n  folders: {\n    \"~/Library/Caches\": true,\n    \"/tmp\": true,\n    \"~/Library/Logs\": true,\n    \"~/Library/Application Support (orphaned)\": true,\n  },\n  privacy: \"standard\",\n  done: true,\n});\n\nconst browser = await chromium.launch();\nconst ctx = await browser.newContext({ viewport: { width: 1280, height: 800 } });\nawait ctx.addInitScript(`try{localStorage.setItem('driftwood.app.v1', '${seed}')}catch(e){}`);\nconst page = await ctx.newPage();\n\nfor (const [name, url, wait] of [\n  [\"welcome\", \"http://localhost:1420/?view=welcome\", 2600],\n  [\"scopes\", \"http://localhost:1420/?view=scopes\", 2600],\n  [\"trust\", \"http://localhost:1420/?view=trust\", 2600],\n  [\"privacy\", \"http://localhost:1420/?view=privacy\", 2600],\n]) {\n  await page.goto(url);\n  await page.waitForTimeout(wait);\n  await page.screenshot({ path: `shots/${name}.png` });\n  console.log(\"shot\", name);\n}\n\n// scan: idle → running → report\nawait page.goto(\"http://localhost:1420/?view=scan\");\nawait page.waitForTimeout(2600);\nawait page.screenshot({ path: \"shots/scan-idle.png\" });\nconsole.log(\"shot scan-idle\");\n\nawait page.click(\"text=Search the river\");\nawait page.waitForTimeout(5000);\nawait page.screenshot({ path: \"shots/scan-running.png\" });\nconsole.log(\"shot scan-running\");\n\nawait page.waitForTimeout(12000);\nawait page.screenshot({ path: \"shots/report.png\" });\nconsole.log(\"shot report\");\n\n// open a reasoning panel\nconst why = page.locator(\"text=Why the river says so\").first();\nawait why.click();\nawait page.waitForTimeout(600);\nawait page.screenshot({ path: \"shots/report-expanded.png\" });\nconsole.log(\"shot report-expanded\");\n\nawait browser.close();\n",
  "filePath": "/var/folders/7g/yz6m8g_s1jj2hffpmwvcwws00000gn/T/kilo/shot.mjs"
}
```
